# From Abba to Zebedee: A Comprehensive Survey of Naming Conventions in the Mishnah, Talmud, and Late Antique Midrash

## v18.1 \- 11-May-2024

## By Ezra Brand

# Abstract {#abstract}

This paper provides an extensive overview of names and naming in early Jewish literature, with a focus on the names of rabbis and other individuals in the community. The study covers a wide range of topics, including given names, diminutives, nicknames, and surnames, as well as titles and honorifics that precede or follow a name. The paper also examines the use of demonyms and gentilics, as well as epithets based on occupation, personal traits, or physical characteristics. Also examined is the relationship between a person's name and their statements or actions. The paper presents charts of personal names and father's names, organized by frequency, providing valuable insights into naming conventions and family relationships in the community. Overall, the paper offers a comprehensive overview of the use of names in early Jewish literature, shedding light on the cultural and social significance of names in this context.

# Keywords {#keywords}

Prosopography; onomastics; toponyms; surnames; epithets; Late Antiquity; Rabbinics; Talmud  
כינוי ; שם לוואי ; שם משפחה ; חז"ל ; תנאים ; אמוראים ; משנה ; תלמוד בבלי ; תלמוד ירושלמי

# Introduction {#introduction}

## Background and Context {#background-and-context}

On the study of names, see [*The Oxford Handbook of Names and Naming*](https://academic.oup.com/edited-volume/34398) (2016): “The study of names, known as ‘onomastics’, is both an old and a young discipline.”  
Satlow et al., “Naming”: “\[T\]here are somewhere in the range of 5,000 individual rabbis named in the literature surveyed by \[TTA\].”   
This massive number is the first thing that makes the challenge. Tied with other factors: The difficult manuscript situation (still no critical editions for many basic works); the complicated history of transmission (oral for hundreds of years); the fact that rabbinic literature is not primarily interested in history, but in didactic teachings.

## Previous Scholarship {#previous-scholarship}

See Katz, “Introduction”.

Most traditional scholarship focuses on biographies.

Regarding works attempting to list the names of Sages, and to provide biographical details of their lives, until the 19th century, it is possible to divide the relevant works into two categories: Those that discuss the Sages in the context of the transmission of the Torah (i.e., in chronological order), and those that provide biographical dictionaries in non-chronological order (usually in alphabetical order).

The earliest lists of Sages were of the first category. The first list of this sort is Masechet Avot. Masechet Avot starts of that “the Torah was given to Moses, who gave it to Yehoshua, who gave it to the Elders (Zekeinim), who gave it to the Prohets (Nevi'im), who gave it to the Men of the Great Assembly (Anshei Kenesset Hagedolah). It then lists the main Sages of each generation, beginning from Shimon Hatzakid (“from the remnants of the K'nesset Hagedolah”, flourished 4th century BCE), until R' Yehuda Hanassi (circa 200 CE). Next to each name is recorded what each Sage “used to say”.

Iggeret Rav Sherira Gaon is meant to provide a history of the transmission.

The first post-Talmudic works in this genre are the anonymous Seder Tanna’im VeAmora’im, and Sherira’s Igeret, in the early medieval period. 

Another pioneering work is Avraham ibn Daud's *Sefer HaKabbalah*.[^1]

The first major work on rabbinic biography was R’ Yehuda b. Kalonymus’s *Yichusei Tannaim VeAmoraim (=YTA).[^2]* Unfortunately, this work was unknown until the 19th century and therefore had no scholarly impact.

The next major work was Avraham Zacut's *Yochasin Hashalem (=SY)*.[^3] R' Yehuda b. Kalynomus and R' Avraham Zacut seem to have independently essentially created hundreds of bios of Tannaim and Amoraim from scratch, based on the Mishnah and Talmudim. Both are monumental works. [^4]

Serious critical scholarship started in the early 19th century. Especially by Weis, *Dor Dor Ve’Dorshav.* Which prompted HaLevy, *Dorot Harishonim.* HaLevy’s work in turn prompted the monumental biographical dictionary by Heiman’s *Toldot Tanaim VeAmoraim* (=TTA), which is still the definitive work, despite its many flaws. 

The advent of computers, and the accompanying open-access digitalization of rabbinic collections, have paved the way for utilizing these resources to make significant progress in this subject. There are also now computerized projects, such as *Otzar HaDemuyot.* Some of which are open-access, crowd-sourced, and ongoing, such as Hebrew *Wikipedia* and *Michlol.* 

## Purpose and Scope of the Study {#purpose-and-scope-of-the-study}

The purpose of this overview is to give a comprehensive survey of naming conventions in rabbinic works in the Late Antique period. Being that the topic is massive, there are a number of aspects that will not be discussed in this survey. Legal and normative aspects of naming will not be discussed.[^5] Discussion of the evolution of naming conventions over time is also not a focus, despite its importance. Names in rabbinic works will mostly be discussed synchronically, not diachronically, except for broad differences between Tannaitic and Amoraic periods and regional variations between Eretz Yisrael and Babylonia.[^6] 

Rather, the focus will be on broadly describing formats of naming and etymology and meaning of names.

# Aptronyms, Word Plays on Names, Patterns within Individual Sages {#aptronyms,-word-plays-on-names,-patterns-within-individual-sages}

## Aptronyms {#aptronyms}

[Aptronym \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aptronym) : “An aptronym, aptonym, or euonym is a personal name aptly or peculiarly suited to its owner.” Nowadays this idea goes under the name of [Nominative determinism \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominative_determinism).   
The Talmud itself states explicitly that some rabbis believed that שמא גרים.[^7] See the following sources (bolding is mine, to highlight the names etymologized): [^8]   
[ירושלמי ראש השנה ג׳:ט׳:ב](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jerusalem_Talmud_Rosh_Hashanah.3.9.2) :   
חד בר נש הוה שמיה כידור. אמר לון רבי מאיר. הובא לכון מיניה. בר נש ביש הוא. כי דור תהפוכות המה.  
בבלי, יומא פג ע"ב :   
ר' מאיר הוה דייק בשמא ר' יהודה ור' יוסי לא הוו דייקו בשמא.  
And see these sources, where R’ Meir is *doresh* names of Biblical figures.  
[בראשית רבה מ״ב:ה](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Bereishit_Rabbah.42.5) :   
עשו מלחמה את ברע וגו' (בראשית יד, ב), רבי מאיר היה דורש שמות, רבי יהושע בן קרחה היה דורש שמות \[...\]  
[רות רבה ב ה](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Ruth_Rabbah.2.5) :   
ושם האיש אלימלך (רות א, ב), רבי מאיר היה דורש שמות. רבי יהושע בן קרחה היה דורש שמות \[...\]  
And here, where Rebbe says that the Biblical name Delilah was apropos:  
בבלי, [סוטה ט׳ ב:טו](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Sotah.9b.15) :   
״ויהי אחרי כן ויאהב אשה בנחל שרק ושמה דלילה״. תניא, רבי אומר: אילמלא לא נקרא שמה **״דלילה״** — ראויה היתה שתקרא דלילה \[...\]  
There appears to often be a connection between names and statements in the Talmud, and wordplay on names. See Shamma Friedman’s article in ואלה השמות on this entire topic. Ornstein discusses specific examples in a number of places.[^9] See also the many examples in Levin, “Midrashei”.[^10] See also R’ Ahikam Kashet’s קובץ יסודות וחקירות, section לשיטתם.[^11] See Inbar, “Diyuk”, p. 15, on the Biblical names Saul and Ish-Bosheth/Mephibosheth, and the Talmudic name R’ Yirmiyah.

## Word Play (Paronomasia) on Name {#word-play-(paronomasia)-on-name}

Bavli, Gittin 7a:   
חסדא שמך וחסדאין מילך  
Bavli, [Gittin 41a](https://www.sefaria.org/Gittin.41a.9):  
אמר רב נחמן בר יצחק: משום דאמי שפיר נאה הוא, אומר שמעתא דלא שפירן?\!   
Bavli, [Sanhedrin 43a](https://www.sefaria.org/Sanhedrin.43a.22) (uncensored text):  
ת"ר חמשה תלמידים היו לו לישו מתאי נקאי נצר ובוני ותודה   
אתיוהו **למתי**   
אמר להו **מתי** יהרג הכתיב (תהלים מב, ג) **מתי** אבוא ואראה פני אלהים   
אמרו לו אין **מתי** יהרג דכתיב (שם מא, ו) **מתי** ימות ואבד שמו   
Apropos the trial of Jesus, the Gemara cites another baraita, where the Sages taught: Jesus the Nazarene had five disciples: Mattai, Nakai, Netzer, Buni, and Toda.   
They brought **Mattai** in to stand trial.   
**Mattai** said to the judges: Shall **Mattai** be executed? But isn’t it written: “When \[***matai***\] shall I come and appear before God?” (Psalms 42:3). Mattai claimed that this verse alludes to the fact he is righteous.   
They said to him: Yes, **Mattai** shall be executed, as it is written: “When \[***matai***\] shall he die, and his name perish?” (Psalms 41:6).  
*Mattai* here is clearly Jesus’ disciple [Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle). The Talmudic story there continues with similar plays on the names of the rest of Jesus' disciples named there: Nakai, Netzer, Buni, and Toda.  
There are a number of other names that are played on in the Talmud: [^15]

1. המנונא  
2. לכלוכית

## Patterns within Individual Sages {#patterns-within-individual-sages}

Important advances in modern scholarship have been made to identify the characteristics of particular sages. Recognizing the differences in personality as reflected in statements has been extensively developed over the past hundred years.[^16] These patterns are most obvious in the differences between Shammai and Hillel and their respective schools.[^17] Previously noted by the traditional commentators are the stylistic flourishes particular to specific Sages.[^18] Richard Kalmin especially discusses patterns to be found in individual Sages, as well as in a generation of Sages. In traditional sources, Heiman often points out patterns in statements. R’ Ahikam Kashet in his *Kovetz Yesodot Vechakirot* lists some of the patterns, calling them “*leshitatam*”.[^19] R' Reuven Margaliyot also often discusses these patterns. In recent biographies of individual Sages, patterns have been pointed out. The *Mishnat Eretz Yisrael* edition of Mishna also often points out patterns.

There are some methodological problems with knowing when something is a pattern. If a certain commonly quoted Sage seems to speak a lot about a certain topic, does not necessarily mean that he had a special interest or expertise in that topic. It may simply be that since that Sage is quoted more often, more of his sayings on that topic are preserved, and the seeming pattern are simply a coincidence.[^20] In order to prove a pattern, one would have to show that of all of that Sages sayings, a greater percentage of them discuss that certain topic, compared to other Sages who speak about it relatively less. Alternatively, one could show that of all the sayings on a certain topic, a larger percentage of them are said by that Sage than the percentage of all his sayings found in Talmudic literature. Of course, such empirical methods are very difficult, if not impossible, especially given the voluminous nature of the Talmud. However, the finding of true patterns seems to be valid in many cases, especially with less common Sages, and where the pattern is found in a relatively large number of cases.[^21] But caution must be exercised.

# Given Names and Their Variations[^22] {#given-names-and-their-variations}

At the outset, it is important to note that individuals during that period typically possessed only one given name.[^23] 

## Variants, Hypocorisms, and Shortenings {#variants,-hypocorisms,-and-shortenings}

There can be a number of reasons for variants:[^24] technical reasons due to transmission, whether oral or manuscript; diminutives/hypocorisms,[^25] and shortenings.  
The Talmud itself discusses variant names, and gives examples, in [Bava\_Batra.166b.2](https://www.sefaria.org/Bava_Batra.166b.2).[^26] 

1. אביי \= אבא  
2. אוריין \= גוריון[^27]  
3. אסי \= איסי (?) \= אסא (?)[^28]  
4. איסי \= יוסף[^29]  
5. בא \= אבא [^30]  
6. בון \= אבון , אבין  
7. גימל \= גמליאל[^31]  
8. דוסא \= דוסתאי[^32]  
9. דימי \= אבדומה[^33]  
10. הונא \= חונא[^34]  
11. ווא \= אבא [^35]  
12. זבדא / זבדי / זבידא \= זבדיה [^36]  
13. חגא \= חגי[^37]  
14. חנן \= חנין[^38]  
15. חנניה \= חנינה[^39]  
16. טבי‎ \= טוביה[^40]  
17. יודן \= יהודה  
18. יוסי \= יוסף  
19. ינאי \= יונתן[^41]  
20. לעזר \= אלעזר[^42]  
21. מניומי \= מנימין (?) \= בנימין (?)[^43]  
22. מנימין \= בנימין[^44]   
23. מתי / מתיי[^45] \= מתתיהו  
24. מתיא \= מתי / מתתיהו [^46]  
25. נחום \= מנחם[^47]  
26. נחוניא \= חוניא[^48]  
27. נחמני \= נחמן[^49]  
28. סימון \= שמעון?  
29. שמן \= שמעון [^50]

## Contraction of Honorific with Given Name {#contraction-of-honorific-with-given-name}

Blending of honorific prefix with given name. [Contraction (grammar) \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contraction_%28grammar%29): “A contraction is a shortened version of the spoken and written forms of a word, syllable, or word group, created by omission of internal letters and sounds.”  
אבימי \= אבא אמי (?)  
אמימר \= אבא מרימר \= אבא מר יימר (?)  
מרימר \= מר יימר  
רבא \= רב אבא  
רבין \= רב אבין  
רבינא \= רב אבינא[^51]  
רבנאי \= רב בנאי /ינאי [^52]  
רמי \= רב אמי  
[רחבה](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%A7%D7%98%D7%92%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%94:%D7%A8%D7%97%D7%91%D7%94) \= רב אהבה (?)  
רפרם \= רב פרם / אפרם [^53]

## No surname {#no-surname}

Where the given name is a common name, and no patronymic or epithet, sometimes the Talmud itself will point out who stam Rabbi X is.   
Many of the cases of rabbis cited without a patronymic or epithet are where someone has a rare given name, since it’s not needed to identify the person.   
Examples of names with no surname: [^61]

## Pseudonyms and Epithets[^68] {#pseudonyms-and-epithets}

See on this Margoliyot, “Study of Names”, chapter 1 (“Pseudonym”, pp. 5-14).[^69]   
The Talmud itself has many instances where it is posited that a Biblical name is a pseudonym.[^70] Common formula:   
"לא X שמו, אלא Y שמו, ולמה נקרא שמו X, ש \[...\]" [^71]  
Example of the Talmud positing that the Tannatic names *Meir* and *Nehorai* (bolding mine):[^72]   
בבלי, [עירובין יג ע"ב](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Eruvin.13b.4):   
"תנא: לא רבי מאיר שמו אלא רבי נהוראי **שמו, ולמה נקרא שמו** רבי מאיר? שהוא מאיר עיני חכמים בהלכה.   
ולא נהוראי שמו אלא רבי נחמיה שמו, ואמרי לה רבי אלעזר בן ערך **שמו, ולמה נקרא שמו** נהוראי? שמנהיר עיני חכמים בהלכה."  
Another two examples of *midrashei shem* of Second Temple figures:   
בבלי, [תענית כ ע" א](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Taanit.20a.4):  
תנא: לא נקדימון שמו, אלא בוני שמו, ולמה נקרא שמו נקדימון, ש\[...\]".  
בבלי, [גיטין נח ע"א](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Gittin.58a.14)  
"אמר ריש לקיש: מעשה באשה אחת וצפנת בת פניאל שמה. צפנת, שהכל צופין ביופיה. בת פניאל, בתו של כהן גדול ששימש לפני ולפנים"  
Another example, about two Tannaim, the first known by seven (\!) different names, the second known by five different names:  
בבלי, [פסחים קיד ע"א \- ע"ב](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Pesachim.113b.18) :  
"תנא, הוא **יוסף איש הוצל**, הוא **יוסף הבבלי**, הוא **איסי בן גור אריה**, הוא **איסי בן יהודה**, הוא **איסי בן גמליאל**, הוא **איסי בן מהללאל**. ומה שמו? — **איסי בן עקביה** שמו.   
הוא **רבי יצחק בן טבלא**, הוא **רבי יצחק בן חקלא**, הוא **רבי יצחק בן אלעא**, הוא **רבי יצחק בן אחא דשמעתא**, הוא **רבי יצחק בן פנחס דאגדתא**. וסימניך (שמעו נא אחיי ורעיי)."  
Another example:   
Examples of likely pseudonyms and epithets:[^73]

## Slurs {#slurs}

Sometimes names are changed by the Talmud, as a negative slur. Other times, patronymics are added (*bar / ben*) to indicate that the person was problematic.[^88]   
Examples:   
אבדן[^89]  
בר אוכמי  
גניבא[^90]

## Different people with same name {#different-people-with-same-name}

One of the major challenges with rabbinic biography.[^91]

### Talmud’s Identification of Anonymous Descriptors[^136]  {#talmud’s-identification-of-anonymous-descriptors}

The Talmud lists a large number of standard formulations used to introduce statements, and their supposed identity, in [Sanhedrin 17b (sections \# 4-6](https://www.sefaria.org/Sanhedrin.17b.4-6)):

1. למידין לפני חכמים \-- לוי מרבי   
2. דנין לפני חכמים \-- שמעון בן עזאי, ושמעון בן זומא, וחנן המצרי, וחנניא בן חכינאי. רב נחמן בר יצחק מתני: חמשה \-- שמעון, שמעון, ושמעון, חנן, וחנניה   
3. רבותינו שבבבל \-- רב ושמואל   
4. רבותינו שבארץ ישראל \-- רבי אבא   
5. דייני גולה \-- קרנא   
6. דייני דארץ ישראל \-- רבי אמי ורבי אסי   
7. דייני דפומבדיתא \-- רב פפא בר שמואל   
8. דייני דנהרדעא \-- רב אדא בר מניומי   
9. סבי דסורא \-- רב הונא ורב חסדא   
10. סבי דפומבדיתא \-- רב יהודה ורב עינא   
11. חריפי דפומבדיתא \-- עיפה ואבימי בני רחבה [^137]  
12. אמוראי דפומבדיתא \-- רבה ורב יוסף   
13. אמוראי דנהרדעי \-- רב חמא  
14. נהרבלאי מתנו \-- רמי בר ברבי   
15. אמרי בי רב \-- רב הונא. והאמר רב הונא "אמרי בי רב"? אלא רב המנונא   
16. אמרי במערבא \-- רבי ירמיה   
17. שלחו מתם \-- ר' יוסי בר חנינא   
18. מחכו עלה במערבא \-- ר' אלעזר 

See also [Bava\_Kamma.103b.11](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Bava_Kamma.103b.11):  
 “ כל היכא דאמרינן ״מעשה בחסיד אחד״ – או רבי יהודה בן בבא, או רבי יהודה ברבי אילעאי”

## Placeholder name {#placeholder-name}

[Placeholder name \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Placeholder_name): “Placeholder names are words that can refer to things or people whose names do not exist, are temporarily forgotten, are not relevant to the salient point at hand, are to avoid stigmatization, are unknowable/unpredictable in the context in which they are being discussed, or are otherwise de-emphasized whenever the speaker or writer is unable to, or chooses not to, specify precisely. Placeholder names for people are often terms referring to an average person or a predicted persona of a typical user.”  
Examples in Talmud:[^138] 

1. חילק ובילק[^139]  
2. יוסף בן שמעון  
3. פלוני[^140]  
4. פלימו[^141]  
5. ראובן, שמעון, לוי, וכו'[^142]

## Demonym  {#demonym}

Some common anonymous descriptors:   
[Demonym \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym):   
“A demonym \[...\] or gentilic \[...\] is a word that identifies a group of people (inhabitants, residents, natives) in relation to a particular place. Demonyms are usually derived from the name of the place\[...\]. Demonyms are used to designate all people (the general population) of a particular place, regardless of ethnic, linguistic, religious or other cultural differences that may exist within the population of that place. \[...\] Since they are referring to territorially defined groups of people, demonyms are semantically different from ethnonyms (names of ethnic groups).”  
Generally a demonym is identical to a toponymic surname. See the common formats for these in the section in this study on “Toponymic Surname”.  
Often these anonymous names are later (likely the Stam, and likely speciously) identified by the Talmud or commentators with known individuals.

### Individual {#individual}

1. בר X[^143]

### Multiple {#multiple}

1. אנשי X  
2. בנות X[^144]  
3. בני X[^145]

Title or honorific preceding name. Generally used as a prefix/title preceding a personal name.[^147]

בריבי[^148]  
רבי (=ר')[^149]  
רב   
רבה[^150]  
רבן[^151]  
רבנא[^152]

## Abba {#abba}

[Abba (given name) \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abba_\(given_name\)) : “Abba is a form of ab, meaning "father" in many Semitic languages. It is used as a given name, but was also used as a title or honorific for religious scholars or leaders. (The word abbot has the same root.)”[^153]  
See Bavli, [Berakhot 16b](https://www.sefaria.org/Berakhot.16b.13):  
תנו רבנן:   
אין קורין ״אבות״ אלא לשלשה,   
ואין קורין ״אמהות״ אלא לארבע \[...\]  
The Sages taught in a baraita:   
One may only call three people patriarchs \[*avot*\], Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, but not Jacob’s children.   
And one may only call four people matriarchs \[*imahot*\], Sarah, Rebecca, Rachel, and Leah \[...\]  
תניא אידך: עבדים ושפחות אין קורין אותם ״אבא פלוני״ ו״אמא פלונית״.   
ושל רבן גמליאל היו קורים אותם ״אבא פלוני״ ו״אמא פלונית״.   
This serves as an introduction; although older people are often referred to with the honorific: Father so-and-so, it was taught in another baraita:   
One may not refer to slaves and maidservants as father \[*abba*\] so-and-so or mother \[*imma*\] so-and-so.   
But they would call the slaves and maidservants of Rabban Gamliel “father so-and-so” and “mother so-and-so.”   
מעשה לסתור?\!   
משום דחשיבי.   
The Gemara asks: Is a story cited in order to contradict the previously stated halakha?\!   
The Gemara answers: There is no contradiction; rather, because Rabban Gamliel’s servants were significant, they were addressed with these honorifics.  
Rashi there:  
"אבא פלוני אמא פלונית – לא בבניהם קאמר אלא בשאר בני אדם כעין שאנו קורין עכשיו מר פלוני מרת פלונית כך היו רגילין לומר אבא פלוני ואמא פלונית"  
It is likely that the famous amora Abayei is a variation of the honorific Abba, and in fact his name was Nahman (diminutive Nahmani).[^154]   
Klein posits that the name “Abba” is a shortening of “Abraham”.[^155] However, it’s more likely that in most, if not all, cases, Abba is not a personal name, but an honorific.

## Aha {#aha}

The name “Aha” (the next most common amoraic personal name, after Abba) may also be an honorific.   
Cf. here, where it’s clearly an honorary prefix:   
[תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/א/אחי שקיא – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%90/%D7%90%D7%97%D7%99_%D7%A9%D7%A7%D7%99%D7%90)  
See here, where the amora named Yitzhak b. Aha is seen as identical to the amora named Yitzhak b. Pinhas:   
פסחים קיד ע"א:   
"רבי יצחק בן אחא דשמעתא הוא רבי יצחק בן פנחס דאגדתא וסימניך (שמעו נא אחיי ורעיי)".   
ורש"י שם:   
רבי יצחק ב"ר אחא דשמעתא \- רבי יצחק סתם האמור באגדתא ר' יצחק בן פנחס הוא:   
שמעו נא אחיי \- דשמעתא בן אחא:

## Imma {#imma}

It is likely that “Imma” is an honorific, like the equivalent male “Abba”. This is posited in the commentary *Yad Eliyahu*, citing R’ Ya’akov Emden.[^174]   
See the name of the daughter of the head of the Sanherin–R’ Shimon b. Gamaliel (making her a member of the Jewish aristocracy):  
[אימא שלום – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9E%D7%90_%D7%A9%D7%9C%D7%95%D7%9D)   
And see Levin, p. 50, for Talmudic wordplay on her name. And see Tal, that the name Shalom for women was extremely common, almost as much so as Miriam / Martha.   
See also:   
אבא שאול בן אמא מרים[^175]  
[תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/א/אימי מרים – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%90/%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9E%D7%99_%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%9D). 

## Pappa {#pappa}

A speculative theory as to why later interpreters came up with the idea of "ten sons of Rav Papa": based on analysis, "Pappa" and "Pappi" are very common as patronyms, but very uncommon as personal names. Maybe they're in fact not names of the father, but a term of honor, like “Abba”?[^176] It is true that Ilan identifies the names Papa-Papi as Iranian, and lists 22 people with these names (the last one from a seal and not from the Bavli). Of the 21 rabbis listed, for 11 it is not a patronym.[^177] However, despite this, the name “Pappa/Pappi” as a first name seems to be far less common than as a patronym.[^178]

## Mar[^179] {#mar}

Mar seems to be more common among later Amoraim, both as honorific (espeically for Reish Galuta), and as a given name.  
As an honorific: 

1.  הונא מר בריה דרבא מפרזקיא \- [Ketubot.10a.13](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Ketubot.10a.13)  
2. [מר זוטרא (פירושונים) – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A8_%D7%96%D7%95%D7%98%D7%A8%D7%90_\(%D7%A4%D7%99%D7%A8%D7%95%D7%A9%D7%95%D7%A0%D7%99%D7%9D\)) \- five amoraim with the name Mar Zutra listed  
3. [מר זוטרא בריה דרב נחמן – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%A7%D7%98%D7%92%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%94:%D7%9E%D7%A8_%D7%96%D7%95%D7%98%D7%A8%D7%90_%D7%91%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%94_%D7%93%D7%A8%D7%91_%D7%A0%D7%97%D7%9E%D7%9F)

5. [מר זוטרא חסידא – המכלול](https://www.hamichlol.org.il/%D7%9E%D7%A8_%D7%96%D7%95%D7%98%D7%A8%D7%90_%D7%97%D7%A1%D7%99%D7%93%D7%90)  
6. [מרימר – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%9E%D7%A8)

## No honorific {#no-honorific}

Many names have no honorific. Some it’s clear why, some not. This is something that R’ Sherira Gaon famously discusses, explaining: גדול מרבן שמו.[^183] Until after the generation of Hillel and Shammai, the title Rabbi was not formalized.[^184]   
Once the honorific *Rabbi* was formalized, it is understood to be problematic if a name is missing that honorific. One common traditional explanation, used already by the Talmud, is that the scholar did not receive *semicha*. This is the explanation for the names בן עזאי ; בן זומא.  
The title מר is assumed to be used when some is not a rabbinical figure, but rather a lay leader. It is especially used for ריש גלותא, in Babylonia.   
Another reason no honorific is given is if the figure is indeed not a rabbi, or if the figure is problematic. The latter seems to be the case for the controversial גניבא.   
A name may be pseudonymous, such as appears to be the case for the rabbi cited with the name איו. [^185] See elsewhere in this study, in section on “Pseudonym”. 

## Contraction of an honorific {#contraction-of-an-honorific}

For some, the name itself is a variant of, or begins with a contraction of, an honorific, as described in other sections. Examples: 

1. אבדן \= אבא (יודן)  
2. אביי \= אבא  
3. אבימי \= אבא (אמי)  
4. אבין \= אבא (אבין)  
5. אמימר \= אבא (יימר)  
6. מרי \= מר  
7. מרימר \= מר (יימר)  
8. רב   
9. רבא[^186] \= רב (אבא?)  
10. רבין \= רב (אבין)  
11. רבינא \= רב (אבינא)  
12. רבנאה[^187]  
13. רבנאי \= רבא  
14. רחבה \= רב (אהבה)  
15. רמי \= רב (אמי)  
16. רפרם \= רב (אפרם / אפרים)

It is likely that the reason is due to their youth, they were not called *Rabbi*. This would explain עיפא well, as he’s one of the חריפי דפומבדיתא, which is apparently a playful term specifically for students in Pumbedita famous for their intellectual sharpness.   
This is likely true also for אושעיא זעירא דמן חבריא, especially according to the explanation that the surname is not toponymic, but should be interpreted as “the youngest of the fellowship”.   
This may be the explanation also for Sages with the name זעירי or זעור, which not be their actual given names, but appelations meaning something like “The Young One”. Examples:  
זעירי  
זעורא בר חמא  
זעור בר חיננא  
For some, no honorific is added because they were considered Sages, or were not respected by the mainstream rabbinic movement. This is the case for the following:   
גניבא .   
Another example is אדא בר אוכמי[^188], who was a *tannai*, and one who was even less respected than a typical *tannai*. He was known to give mistaken readings of texts.[^189]  
When the name is only a patronymic (i.e., starting with *ben / bar*), it never starts with an honorific. This is true for the Sage ברדלא as well, which is a contraction of בר דלא.  
Tekonymic names (=referring to father by the name of his son, see more on this later) have no honorific as well, such as אבוה דפלוני.   
Of course, where the person is not a rabbi, but a layperson, there’s no honorific. Examples: 

1. טבות רישבא

Other figures with there’s no “rabbi” honorific, where it’s unclear why:[^190] 

1. אביטול ספרא  
2. אגרא חמיו של רבי אבא  
3. אבוה בר איהי  
4. אהבה בריה דרבי זירא  
5. אילפא  
6. אמי אבוה דשמואל בר אמי  
7. אמי ורדנאה  
8. אשיין בר נדבך  
9. באלי  
10. גמליאל זוגא  
11. הון בן רב נחמן[^191]  
12. זבדא בן לוי  
13. זוגא דקיסרין  
14. חזקיה בן רבי חייא  
15. חייא בר רב  
16. יהודה בירבי  
17. יהודה בן בוני  
18. יהודה ברבי חייא  
19. יהודה בר גרוגרות  
20. יהודה מר בר מרימר  
21. יוחנן בר מריה  
22. יוסי בר מנשיא  
23. יושוע בר גיזורה  
24. יעקב איש כפר נבוריא  
25. יעקב קרחה  
26. יצחק בן גופתא  
27. לוי בריה דרב הונא בר חייא  
28. לוי בר שמואל  
29. לוי סוכיה  
30. מנימין בר איהי  
31. מתתיה בן יהודה  
32. נחמיה בר ברוך  
33. עולא[^192]  
34. עולא ביראה  
35. עולא בר חיננא  
36. עולא בר רב  
37. עולא רבה  
38. עולא שכפא  
39. קרנא  
40. שילא מרי  
41. שמואל  
42. שמואל הזקן  
43. שמעון בר חייא

The name עולא seems to be over-represented in this lists. Maybe the name עולא is itself a kind of honorific, like אבא or מר ? It could be that it’s related to the word עילאי, meaning “elite; elevated”.[^193] 

# Surnames and Their Origins {#surnames-and-their-origins}

## Etiological Explanations Provided by the Talmud {#etiological-explanations-provided-by-the-talmud}

Talmudic literature offers etiological explanations for various Talmudic surnames. The explanation mostly consists of dramatic stories.  
Examples of surnames etymologized by the Talmud: [^194]

1. המעגל[^195]  
2. (בן) כלבא שבוע  
3. (שמואל) הקטן ([שמואל הקטן – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9C_%D7%94%D7%A7%D7%98%D7%9F): "לפשר כינויו "הקטן" מביא התלמוד הירושלמי שני הסברים: "לפי שהיה מקטין עצמו" ונוהג בענווה, ו"לפי שמעט (בלבד) היה קטן משמואל הרמתי" )  
4. איש גמזו[^196]  
5. בן ציצית הכסת  
6. בעל כנפיים[^197]  
7. בר כוכבא  
8. הנחבא[^198]  
9. מוקיר שבי[^199]  
10. קטנתה / קטנתא / קטנותא [^200]

See also in this study, in the section “Pseudonym”. 

## Patronymic and Other Familial Relations {#patronymic-and-other-familial-relations}

### Patronymic {#patronymic}

[Patronymic \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic):  
“A patronymic, or patronym, is a component of a personal name based on the given name of one's father, grandfather (avonymic), or an earlier male ancestor.”

בר X  
בן X  
ברבי X [^201]  
בריה דX  
בנו של[^202]

### Patronymic, Not Father's Name {#patronymic,-not-father's-name}

See my paper on the topic: “[Ben Bag Bag, Ben He He, and Beyond: Patronymics In Hazal Which Are Not Father's Names](https://www.academia.edu/97559307/Ben_Bag_Bag_Ben_He_He_and_Beyond_Patronymics_In_Hazal_Which_Are_Not_Fathers_Names)”.

### Patronymic Alone, No Given Name[^203] {#patronymic-alone,-no-given-name}

Meaning, where a person is typically referred to by patronymic alone. Including where a given name is known, but is not typically used.[^204] It’s clearly a more informal way of referring to somebody.[^205] Seems especially common for less rabbinic figures in Tannaitic and Temple times. Some instances seem to be pseudonyms, such as בן הא הא and בן בג בג.   
Examples:

1. בן בג בג  
2. בן הא הא  
3. בן דמה[^206]  
4. בן בוכרי / כוברי [^207]  
5. בן זומא   
6. בן עזאי  
7. בן פטורי / פטורא / פתורא[^208]  
8. בן רחומי[^209]  
9. בן שלקות[^210]  
10. בר אהינא[^211]  
11. בר הדיא[^212]

14. בר חטייא[^215]

### Matronymic {#matronymic}

[Matronymic \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matronymic):  
“A matronymic is a personal name based on the given name of one's mother, grandmother, or any female ancestor. It is the female equivalent of a patronymic. Around the world, matronymic surnames are far less common than patronymic surnames. In some cultures in the past, matronymic last names were often given to children of unwed mothers. Or if a woman was especially well known or powerful, her descendants might adopt a matronym based on her name.”

1. בן אימא מרים[^230]  
2. בן החורנית[^231]  
3. בן פזי[^232]  
4. בר מרייה / מרייא [^233]  
5. בר מרתא[^234]  
6. בר רחל[^235]

### Papponymic  {#papponymic}

[Papponymic \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papponymic):   
“A papponymic is a personal name based on the name of one's grandfather, similar to a patronymic, a name derived from the name of one's father.”  
בר בר[^236]  
בר ברתיה[^237]

### Teknonymy {#teknonymy}

[Teknonymy \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teknonymy):   
“Teknonymy \[...\] is the practice of referring to parents by the names of their children.”  
אבוה / אבוי דX[^238]

## Toponymic Surnames {#toponymic-surnames}

[Toponymic surname \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponymic_surname):   
“A toponymic surname or topographic surname is a surname derived from a place name. This can include specific locations, such as the individual's place of origin, residence \[...\]”.  
See also [Patrial name \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrial_name): “A patrial name or geographical surname is a surname or second cognomen given to \[a\] person deriving from a toponym, the name for a geographical place. It was particularly common in ancient Greece and Rome. The lack of a family surname and use of a geographical cognomen as a surname may the sign of a lower class, such as courtesans when signing a contract.[^245] With medieval writers, Christian, Jewish and Muslim, whose works circulated around many countries, geographical cognomens sometimes served to distinguish better than "son of," "ben" or "ibn."”.  
See Klein, p. 349 and on.[^246]  
The Mishna uses the Biblical format of *‘ha-X-i’* , but more often uses the format of *‘ish X’*:[^247]  
הXי   
איש X  
The Talmud Bavli typically uses the formats of:  
מX  
דX  
Talmud Yerushalmi, more than the others, uses format of:   
דמן X  
Xיא  
For the toponyms mentioned in the following surnames, see my “[An Annotated List of Toponyms Mentioned in the Talmud](https://www.academia.edu/118899020/An_Annotated_List_of_Toponyms_Mentioned_in_the_Talmud)”.

## Double Names \- Hebrew \+ Greek  {#double-names---hebrew-+-greek}

When a person had a Hebrew given name, and also a non-Hebrew name. [^544]  
Example:   
מנחם טלמא [^545]  
רבי שמעון יאסיניא[^546]  
It appears that in many names that appear in patronymic format (“ben/bar X”), the patronym is in fact the Greek nickname of the individual. Meaning, the individual himself, not the father.

# Analysis of Given Names {#analysis-of-given-names}

## Given Names and Fathers' Names {#given-names-and-fathers'-names}

### All Given names, by frequency[^574] {#all-given-names,-by-frequency}

Names of rabbis in Hazal are around a third Hebrew, two-thirds Aramaic (see Ilan). And this includes the Mishnah, where a far higher percentage of names are of Hebrew origin. So in the Talmuds, the percentage of names of Aramaic origin is even higher than two-thirds.[^575]   
There are only very few Greek (even in the Jerusalem Talmud) or Iranian names (even in the Babylonian Talmud).

## Male Jewish names, by etymology {#male-jewish-names,-by-etymology}

### Hebrew origin {#hebrew-origin}

Bareket, “Name”:   
“Insofar as Hebrew names, certain seemingly central biblical names virtually disappeared (Abraham, David, Solomon, Moses, Aaron), while others remained in use (Judah, Eliezer, Elyakim, Jacob, Joseph, Joshua, Jonathan, etc.). Non-biblical Hebrew names were also in use (Mattathias, Gamliel,[^579] Nahman, Meir).”  
Kaganoff, “Names”:   
“Throughout the later books of the Bible, in Ezra, Nehemiah, and Chronicles, the old Hebrew names of the patriarchal era—Joseph, Benjamin, Simeon-reappear \[...\]  
During the period of Alexander’s rule, new Hebrew names continued to be invented. Examples of these occur in the early Mishnayoth: Shatach,[^580] Admon, Perachiah.”  
In this section, labels “Tanaim” and “Amoraim” mean “Tannaitic period” and “Amoraic period”.[^581] It is based on quantitative analysis of the respective Wikipedia categories. Includes both given names as well as patronyms.   
Of all of these Hebrew names, only the name Tanchum is truly not in the Bible. All the other ones are simply variants of Biblical names, indicated by an equals sign (“=”). As noted earlier, the name "Yossi" is a diminutive form of "Yosef", but "Yossi" as such is not mentioned in the Bible. As can be seen, some names are found only in one period:   
Only in Tannaitic period: Gamliel ; Yishmael ; Hanania. Only in Amoraic period: Yitzchak ; Yermiah ; Berechia.

2. בנימין [^583]  
3. ברכיה [^584]

### Aramaic origin, with possible etymology {#aramaic-origin,-with-possible-etymology}

Bareket, “Name”:   
“Aramaic names \[...\] began to appear already in the Persian period \[and\] remain part of the repertoire well into the Mishnaic and Talmudic eras, (Abba, Akiva, Nehorai, Chiya, Shemaiah), perhaps even gaining in popularity over time.”  
Kaganoff, “Names”:   
“We find many names in Ezra, Nehemiah, and Chronicles with the Aramaic ending ‘*ai’*. Even names that have a Hebrew root are converted into an Aramaic form, as for example: Piltai (from Pelatiah), Shammai (from Shemaiah), Atlai (from Ataliah), and a name which has been much in the public eye, Adlai (from Adaliah). We also come across names entirely and directly Aramaic, as well as names borrowed by the Jews from neighboring peoples. Persian-Babylonian names, brought back from the Exile, make their first appearance at this time. Two names in this last category, Mordecai and Shabbetai, have remained popular to this day despite their pagan origin.”

64.  רבה / רבא [^687]\- Contraction of רב אבא. Alternatively, related to Rabba, meaning “Great”  
65. [^688]רבינא \- Contractions of רב אבינא. Alternatively, related to “Great” \- רבא  
66. רמי [^689]\- Contraction of רב אמי  
67. שילא[^690]  
68. שימי[^691]  
69. תחליפא \- [^692]

### Fathers’ names, by frequency {#fathers’-names,-by-frequency}

Not all “patronyms” are in fact names of fathers. Many are surnames. For example, in the name שמעון בן שטח. See on this Ilan in her intro.  
Highlighted yellow below are cases where a name is quite common as a “patronym” (in the broad sense, of any name where the construction is “son of X”), but relatively rare as a first name. This might indicate that this is not actually the father’s name, but is a different type of surname.

### Greek and Latin origin {#greek-and-latin-origin}

Bareket, “Name”, writes:  
“After the conquest of Alexander, and years of Greek and then Roman rule, Greek became a dominant language in the Levant. Thus, in the Hellenistic period we begin to see the use of Greek names, such as Menelaus, Jason, Antipater, Aristobolus, etc. We also find some rabbis with Greek names, (Antigonus, Alexandri, etc.), but only infrequently.”  
Kaganoff, “Names”:  
“\[..I\]n the Mishnah and Talmudic literatures we find many Tannaim and Amoraim with such Greek names as Alexander, Theodorus, Dosa, Nanos,[^694] Nakdimon, Polemo, Pappos, Patros, Symmachos, Tarfon, etc.  
Following the Roman conquest of Palestine, a strong Latin influence made itself felt, though it was never so extensive as the Greek. In the Mishnaic and Talmudic literatures (especially the Palestinian Talmud) we encounter many rabbis with Latin names: Drusus, Marinus, Valens, Romanus, Justus, and, mirabile dictu, in several places in the Palestinian Talmud mention is made of a Rabbi Titus\! There were Jews who even bore the names of the pagan Roman gods, Apollo and Castor.”  
The Talmud Bavli in Gittin is aware that many names are indistinguishable from non-Jew’s names. In a sugya based on a conversation between R’ Yochanan and Reish Lakish:   
Bavli, [Gittin 11a-11b](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Gittin.11a.13-11b.5) (bolding is mine):  
\[...\] לא בא לידינו אלא לוקוס ולוס, והכשרנו.   
ודוקא לוקוס ולוס, דלא שכיחי ישראל דמסקי בשמהתייהו, אבל שמהתא אחריני דשכיחי ישראל דמסקי בשמהתייהו – לא.   
\[...\] שרוב ישראל שבחוצה לארץ שמותיהן כשמות גוים.   
Rabbi Yoḥanan said to him: There came before us bills of divorce that were signed only with names such as Lukos and Los \[...\]   
The Talmud qualifies:  
\[S\]pecifically \[...\] names such as Lukos and Los, as it is uncommon to find Jews who are called by these names. However, with regard to other gentile names, concerning which it is common to find Jews who are called by these names \[...\]  
Then, quoting the Tosefta:   
\[...\] \[T\]he names of most Jews outside of Eretz Yisrael are like the names of gentiles.  
Examples:[^695]

1. אבדימי / אבדומא / אבודימא / אבדימוס / אבדימס (דימי?) / (אבימי?) [^696]

### Persian origin {#persian-origin}

See Bavli, [Gittin 11a](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Gittin.11a.2):   
היכי דמי שמות מובהקין? אמר רב פפא, כגון: הורמיז, ואבודינא, בר שיבתאי, ובר קידרי, ובאטי, ונקים אונא.  
The Gemara clarifies: What are the circumstances of unambiguous gentile names? Rav Pappa said: This is referring to names such as Hurmiz, and Abbudina, bar Shibbetai, and bar Kidri, and Bati, and Nakim Una.  
Yaakov Elman writes: “It should be recalled that R. Papa knows the difference between names that are used by both Jews and non-Jews and names that are unambiguously non-Jewish – Hannez, Abudina, Bar Shibtai, Bar Qidri, Bati, Naqim, and Una (B. Gittin 11a).”[^735]

In terms of *Yochasin*'s generations of Amoraim, presumably this was based on *Sefer HaKabbalah*.

A synchronic approach \[...\] considers a language at a moment in time without taking its history into account. Synchronic linguistics aims at describing a language at a specific point of time, often the present. In contrast, a diachronic \[...\] approach, as in historical linguistics, considers the development and evolution of a language through history.” For a more diachronic approach, see Ilan.

    ׳ארדא׳ ו׳ארטא׳ ו׳פילי בריש׳

[^12]:  See also the ואלה השמות article on the etymology of the Biblical name גד, and the rabbinic awareness of it.  
    It should be pointed out that in general, interest within Jewish literature in etymology of names can be traced to the Bible, particularly the Book of Genesis. This interest focuses on the origins of names for people and places. Examples include the names of Adam, Eve, the Patriarchs, and the 12 tribes.  
    See:   
     [מדרש שם – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%93%D7%A8%D7%A9_%D7%A9%D7%9D) ; משה גרסיאל, *מדרשי שמות במקרא*, תשמ"ח.  
    Interesting and unusual case of a Biblical etymology not of place name or person name is that of the word Manna \- מן.  
    The Talmud further explores the etymology of many Biblical names, especially when connecting obscure figures to known figures, or otherwise harmonizing different names. Examples from the Talmud include the names of Mount Sinai, King Solomon, Moses, Jethro, and others.   
    See Steinzaltz on Megillah 13a (bolding mine), where R’ Shimon ben Pazi gives an extreme conflation, saying that all the names in a verse are in fact referring to one individual \-- Moses: “This introduction \[by R’ Shimon ben Pazi to his exposition of the book of Chronicles\] made reference to the fact that the book of Chronicles cannot always be interpreted literally but requires exposition, **as the same individual might be called by various different names**.”  
    Some examples (see also examples in this study for Talmudic names, in the section “Pseudonyms”):   
    Names of people conflated with others, and etymologized:

    1. ברכות ד ע"א: כרתי ופלתי \= סנהדרין ; מפיבשת \= איש בשת ; כלאב \= דניאל  
    2. עירובין נג ע"א: אמרפל \= נמרוד  
    3. ראש השנה כה ע"א: ירובעל \= גדעון ; בדן \= שמשון  
    4. סנהדרין יט ע"ב \- כא ע"א: מרד \= כלב ; פלטי \= פלטיאל ; עגלה \= מיכל  
    5. סנהדרין פב ע"ב: "א"ר יוחנן: חמשה שמות יש לו \-- זמרי, ובן סלוא, ושאול, ובן הכנענית, ושלומיאל בן צורי שדי"  
    6. מגילה יב ע"ב \- טו ע"א: ממוכן \= המן ; אסתר \= הדסה ; יסכה \= שרה ; יהודיה \= בתיה ; מלאכי \= מרדכי ; התך \= דניאל. וראו שם ששה שמות נוספים של משה (ירד ; גדור ; חבר ; סוכו ; יקותיאל ; זנוח)  
    7. סוטה יא ע"ב \- יב ע"א: שפרה \= יוכבד ; פועה \= מרים ; אשחור \= כלב  
    8. סוטה מב ע"ב: שופך \= שובך ; הרפה \= ערפה  
    9. בבא בתרא צא ע"ב: שרף ויואש \= מחלון וכליון; יוקים \= יהושע ; כזבא \= גבעון

    Names of places eymologized:

    1. טבריה ; חמת ; רקת ; כינרת ; ציפורי (בבלי, מגילה ה ע"ב \- ו ע"א. ראו אורנשטיין, עמ' 1128, ערך "חמת". ראו ציטוט מר' יעקב עמדין שם על התופעה בכלל)  
    2. חדקל (אורנשטיין, עמ' 1053, ערך "חד-קל")  
    3. ירדן (בבלי, בכורות נה ע"א)  
    4. הר סיני (שבת פט ע"א \- ע"ב: "חמשה שמות יש לו")  
    5. פיתום ; רעמסס (סוטה יא ע"א)

    For more examples, and full sources, see my “[“Why Was He Called Thus?”: An Anthology of Talmudic Passages Relating to Explanations of Biblical Names, Unification of Ostensibly Separate Biblical Personalities, and Etymologies of Biblical Words](https://www.academia.edu/110234164/_Why_Was_He_Called_Thus_An_Anthology_of_Talmudic_Passages_Relating_to_Explanations_of_Biblical_Names_Unification_of_Ostensibly_Separate_Biblical_Personalities_and_Etymologies_of_Biblical_Words)”. For other previous scholarship on word play on Biblical names in rabbinic literature, see Moshe Garsiel, *Biblical Names: A Literary Study of Midrashic Derivations and Puns* (1991), and Shamir Yona, “[Rhetorical Features in Talmudic Literature](https://www.academia.edu/6393066/Rhetorical_Features_in_Talmudic_Literature_HUCA_77_)”, *Hebrew Union College Annual*, Vol. 77 (2006), pp. 84; 90-1.

    It is likely that the Talmud was prompted in this, since the name Meir is unusual. In fact, no other Sage has this name.

[^14]:  תענית דף כ ע"א: "תנא לא נקדימון שמו אלא בוני שמו ולמה נקרא שמו נקדימון ש\[...\]"  
    It is likely that the Talmud found the Greek name Nakdimon to be unusual to prompt this.

[^16]: 	See C. Gafni, *P'shuto,* pg. 33, footnote 55, that until the end of the 19th century, there was very little consciousness of the different methodologies of individual sages. At the end of that century, scholars became more conscious of the individuality of the various Sages. Interestingly, this methodology was quickly adopted even by traditionalists, such as the Aderet (brought in R’ Kashet, *Kovetz Yesodot Vechakirot*), and Heiman in many places (e.g., by R' Yehuda)

[^17]:  One can posit that the Mishnah already distinguished the propensity of Beis Shammai to be strict, when it lists the the kulot of Beis Shammai in Eduyot, clearly showing that this is the exception. However, this doesn't mean that the Mishnah felt that there was a significant pattern, rather it is possible that the Mishnah saw Beis Shammai being generally *machmir* a coincidence, and listed his Kulot because it was technically simpler. The Talmud also will often point out a contradiction within the opinions of a certain Sage; that in one place he is *meikel* and one place *machmir* (examples?), but it would appear that questions of this sort only appear regarding opinions of a Sage *within a certain topic.* Clearly, the Talmud is assuming that the Sage is *meikel* or *machmir* for halachic reasons, and not because he is generally more meikil or machmir. Of course, even if we posit that a Sage is generally more strict or lenient, it is possible that this is also for a halachic reason. For example, it could be that he does it because “lifnim mishurat hadin”.   
    However, it still must be explained why that Sage follows “*lifnim mishurat hadin*” more than any other Sage. Ultimately there must be one of two explanations: One, For social reasons. It could be that the Sage is generally more strict or lenient because his Rebbe is, or because the surrounding culture is (Jewish or non-Jewish), or because his family is, etc. Two, the Sage because of his personality is more conducive to being strict or lenient. (The Arizal seems to go with the second approach, when he writes that Beis Shammai's neshama comes from din. In the future, he says we will follow Beis Shammai, because we will live in a world of din. This is along the lines of the second reason mentioned.)  
    Another methodological consideration must be pointed out. When there is no pattern exhibited by a certain Sage to be either more strict or lenient, and a statement is explained on its own, it is obviously impossible to prove that the statement comes from the second reason, viz., that his personality causes him to tend towards one extreme. Since that sage's views sometimes fall in one camp and sometimes in the other, it is obviously impossible to decide whether his personality caused him to be strict or not. If anything, we see that personality-wise, the Sage is even-handed. Also possible is that the Sage ignored his personal feelings and emotions when deciding halacha. Of course, it is possible that half the statements of a particular Sage represent his true personality, and the other half are cases where he ignored his feelings, but with no external evidence, it is impossible to know. However, if there is a clear story showing the Sage's personality, half the statements can be explained based on his personality. However, the essential fact that half the cases go against his personality would seem to imply that the explanation is possibly faulty. 

[^18]: 	For example, the expression “*gadif*” to R' Achai.

[^19]: 	I have not used the word *leshitatam* (“according to his opinion”), which implies a *halachic* opinion. I have instead used the word “pattern”, whose connotation, I feel, better includes social and personality factors.

[^22]:  General rule for citations: I cite entries in the following order of preference: Hebrew Wikipedia; HaMichlol; TTA; H

[^23]:  For a discussion, based on traditional sources, see here:   
    [רבי יהושע בן פתר ראש – המכלול](https://www.hamichlol.org.il/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%99%D7%94%D7%95%D7%A9%D7%A2_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%A4%D7%AA%D7%A8_%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%A9)  
    The practice of having two names emerged during the medieval period, initially gaining popularity among the aristocracy. See [Middle name \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_name) “Among royalty and aristocracy, middle names have been used since the late 17th century (and possibly earlier)\[...\]”. Regarding ancient naming conventions, see [Roman naming conventions \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_naming_conventions): “The distinguishing feature of Roman nomenclature was the use of both personal names and regular surnames. Throughout Europe and the Mediterranean, other ancient civilizations distinguished individuals through the use of single personal names, usually dithematic \[=consisting of two stems\] in nature. Consisting of two distinct elements, or "themes", these names allowed for hundreds or even thousands of possible combinations. But a markedly different system of nomenclature arose in Italy, where the personal name was joined by a hereditary surname. Over time, this binomial system expanded to include additional names and designations.”  
    Cf. also Edwin D. Lawson, “Personal Naming Systems”, *The Oxford Handbook of Names and Naming*, pp. 9-10:   
    “The original personal name system of the German-speaking countries (Germany, Austria,and part of Switzerland) is similar to that of other Germanic and most Indo-European Name systems. Until the twelfth century people bore a single name. Most names consisted of two word-stems \[...\] Originally the inventory of names was very extensive because the word-stems were continually used for new combinations. Later on they were no longer understood and the formation of new names ceased. \[...\] In the twelfth century a twofold revolution took place. Inspired by Romance customs and favoured by the development of administration, surnames came to be used \[...\] By the end of the fifteenth century most people had fixed surnames.”  
    And on p. 14: “Rabbi Judah He-Hassid (1150–1217) influenced naming customs among Ashkenazic Jewry by ruling that: children should be named only after the deceased; one should not marry a person with the same name as one’s parents. These restrictions may have led to the greater frequency among Ashkenazic Jewry of giving a child two Hebrew names \[...\] Double names found especially among Yiddish speaking Eastern European Jewry were generally a sacred Hebrew anthroponym and a secular Yiddish translation or complementary name.”

[^24]:  On variants in names in Hazal, see Katz, “Introduction”, pp. 24-25.

[^25]:  [Hypocorism \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypocorism): “A hypocorism \[...\] or pet name is a name used to show affection for a person. It may be a diminutive form of a person's name, such as Izzy for Isabel or Bob for Robert, or it may be unrelated.”

    מאי שנא שתי אותיות דלא – דלמא מיתרמי שם בן ארבע אותיות, והוה ליה פלגיה דשמא; אי הכי, אות אחת נמי – דלמא מיתרמי שם בן שתי אותיות, והוה ליה פלגיה דשמא\!   
    אלא שתי אותיות היינו טעמא – דלמא מיתרמי שם בן שלש אותיות, והוה ליה רובא דשמא.

    "תנו רבנן: למה לא נתפרשו שמותם של זקנים הללו? שלא יאמר אדם: פלוני כמשה ואהרן' פלוני כנדב ואביהוא? פלוני כאלדד ומידד?"

[^88]:  This was understood to be the case for Bar Kochba, sometimes referred to in Talmudic sources as בר כוזיבא, which seems to be negative. However, modern archeological findings confirmed that his name was in fact בר כוסיבא, and that בר כוכבא was likely a positive nickname, based on a Biblical verse (“דרך כוכב מיעקב”). It may be, however, that בר כוסיבא is changed to בר כוזיבא as a slur. 

[^89]:  Not definitely a slur. The name is a contraction of אבא יודן.

[^92]:  "זקן אחד" \- אורנשטיין, ערך "זקן אחד", עמ' 972 ואילך.   
    "חסיד אחד" \- אורנשטיין, עמ' 1151\. ראו שם ציטוט מהתלמוד: "כל היכא דמאמרינן מעשה בחסיד אחד, או ר' יהודה בן בבא או ר' יהודה בר' עילאי."  
    The phrase משום זקן אחד [appears seven times in the Talmud](https://www.sefaria.org/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A9%D7%95%D7%9D%20%D7%96%D7%A7%D7%9F%20%D7%90%D7%97%D7%93&tab=text&tpathFilters=Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=relevance&svar=1&ssort=relevance).  
    In [Berakhot.55b.16](https://www.sefaria.org/Berakhot.55b.16), the Elder is identified as R’ Bana’ah:  
    אמר רבי ביזנא בר זבדא, אמר רבי עקיבא, אמר רבי פנדא, אמר רב נחום, אמר רבי בירים, משום זקן אחד, ומנו — רבי בנאה \[...\]  
    That statement is also highly unusual for having six \[\!\] tradents cited in chain, instead of the usual two or three. Four of the names are highly unusual: R’ Bizna bar Zavda, R’ Panda, Rav Nahum, and R’ Biriyim.  
    In [Chullin.98a.9](https://www.sefaria.org/Chullin.98a.9), the Elder is identified as R’ Ya’akov:  
    אמר רב שמן בר אבא, אמר רב אידי בר אידי בר גרשם, אמר לוי בר פרטא, אמר רבי נחום, אמר רבי ביריים, משום זקן אחד \-- ורבי יעקב שמיה, דבי נשיאה אמרו \[...\]  
    Here, seven (\!) tradents are cited in the chain, and again we have the exact same chain of Rav Nahum citing R’ Biriyim citing the Elder. It is likely that the Elder is identified by the gloss of the anonymous Talmud as R’ Ya’akov, because R’ Ya’akov was a member of the Nasi’s household.  
    In [Bava\_Kamma.82a.2](https://www.sefaria.org/Bava_Kamma.82a.2), the Elder is identified as R’ Yehoshua ben Levi:  
    ר' תנחום ור' ברייס אמרי משום זקן אחד, ומנו \-- ר' יהושע בן לוי  
    This sugya is the only time that R’ Berayes appears in the Talmud; he’s cited again later in that sugya in the same format, [Bava\_Kamma.81a.9](https://www.sefaria.org/Bava_Kamma.81a.9):  
    ר' תנחום ור' ברייס משום זקן אחד  
    This format is especially common at the beginning of stories (many of these formulas multiple times in the Talmud, for these, I added hyperlink to Sefaria search, in Mishnah and Talmud, for exact match, manually counting number of results)):   
    [מעשה באחד](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%91%D7%90%D7%97%D7%93&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) (46 תוצאות)  
    [מעשה באדם אחד](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%91%D7%90%D7%93%D7%9D%20%D7%90%D7%97%D7%93&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) (32 תוצאות)  
    [מעשה באשה אחת](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%91%D7%90%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%90%D7%97%D7%AA&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) (26 תוצאות)  
    [מעשה בחסיד אחד](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%91%D7%97%D7%A1%D7%99%D7%93%20%D7%90%D7%97%D7%93&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) (16 תוצאות)  
    (See [Bava\_Kamma.103b.11](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Bava_Kamma.103b.11) \- “ כל היכא דאמרינן ״מעשה בחסיד אחד״ – או רבי יהודה בן בבא, או רבי יהודה ברבי אילעאי”)  
    [מעשה בתלמיד אחד](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%A9%D7%94%20%D7%91%D7%AA%D7%9C%D7%9E%D7%99%D7%93%20%D7%90%D7%97%D7%93&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) (11 תוצאות)

    Cf. [Banu (Arabic) \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_%28Arabic%29) : “Banu is Arabic for "the children of" or "descendants of" and appears before the name of a tribal progenitor. The English counterpart would be "House of" \[...\] "Bani Israel" is a common Arabic word for the children of Israel; a Quranic chapter is named so. The term itself is very close to its Hebrew counterpart: B'nei Yisrael' (בני ישראל).”

[Hasideans \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasideans): “The Hasideans (Hebrew: חסידים הראשונים, Hasidim ha-Rishonim, Greek Ἀσιδαῖοι or Asidaioi, also transcribed as Hasidaeans and Assideans) were a Jewish group during the Maccabean Revolt that took place from around 167–142 BCE. The Hasideans are mentioned three times in the books of the Maccabees, the main contemporary sources from the period\[...\] The Hasideans have been the object of much scholarly speculation: were they a specific group with a coherent ideology, or merely a generic term for "the faithful"?”

[^124]:  "רבי יוסי אומר: **משפחת בית פגרים** ו**בית ציפרא** מעמאום היו משיאין לכהונה" \- [Arakhin.10a.4](https://www.sefaria.org/Arakhin.10a.4)  
    "אבל אני מעיד לכם על שתי משפחות גדולות שהיו בירושלים, **משפחת בית צבועים** מבן עכמאי, **ומשפחת בית קופאי** מבן מקושש, שהם בני צרות, ומהם כהנים גדולים, ושמשו על גבי המזבח. " \- [Yevamot.15b.7](https://www.sefaria.org/Yevamot.15b.7)  
    "רבן שמעון בן גמליאל אומר: מין בהמה היא, ושל **בית דושאי** היו מגדלין מהן עדרים עדרים" \- [Chullin.80a.12](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Chullin.80a.12)  
    "תני, ר' יוסי אומר: של **בית חגירה** כותבנים אומנים היו בירושלם \[...\]" \- [Jerusalem\_Talmud\_Megillah.1.9.17](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jerusalem_Talmud_Megillah.1.9.17). [תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/ב/בית חגירה](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%91/%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA_%D7%97%D7%92%D7%99%D7%A8%D7%94)  
    ראו גם: "אמרה לו: רבי, מ**משפחת דורקטי** אני" \- [Ketubot.10b.4](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Ketubot.10b.4)  
    However, it appears that that’s not a proper family name, but a generic term, cf. [Jerusalem\_Talmud\_Ketubot.1.1.15](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jerusalem_Talmud_Ketubot.1.1.15): “עליה להביא ראייה שהיא מ**משפחת טרוקטי**.” And see Jastrow: “[דּוֹרְקְטִי](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jastrow%2C_%D7%93%D6%BC%D7%95%D6%B9%D7%A8%D6%B0%D7%A7%D6%B0%D7%98%D6%B4%D7%99) f. (a perversion of τρωκτή \[[*trōktos*](https://lsj.gr/wiki/%CF%84%CF%81%CF%89%CE%BA%CF%84%CF%8C%CF%82)\], sub. Σταφυλή \[[*staphulḗ*](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CF%83%CF%84%CE%B1%CF%86%CF%85%CE%BB%CE%AE)\], v. infra) grape used for dessert, fit for eating but yielding no wine, fig. a woman who has no menstruation. ”

[^125]:  See especially [Mishnah\_Ta'anit.4.5](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Mishnah_Ta'anit.4.5), for an interesting historical list of Second Temple clans:  
    בני ארח  
    בני דוד  
    בני פרעש  
    בני יונדב בן רכב  
    בני סנאה  
    בני זתוא \[...\] בני גונבי עלי \[...\] בני קוצעי קציעות  
    בני פחת מואב  
    בני עדין  
    And this is common for the household of the *nasi*:  
    [בית רבן גמליאל](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA%20%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%9F%20%D7%92%D7%9E%D7%9C%D7%99%D7%90%D7%9C&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud|Tosefta&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance) \- "של בית רבן גמליאל ושל בית רבי חנניא סגן הכהנים היו \[...\]" \- [Mishnah\_Shekalim.6.1](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Mishnah_Shekalim.6.1)  
    [בית רבי](https://www.sefaria.org.il/search?q=%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA%20%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99&tab=text&tpathFilters=Mishnah|Talmud|Tosefta&tvar=0&tsort=chronological&svar=1&ssort=relevance)  
    This is the common construct of family name in the Bible.   
    On the formula of of “b’nei X” for a family or clan, compare [Banu (Arabic) \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_\(Arabic\)):  
    Banu (بنو) is Arabic for "the children of" or "descendants of" and appears before the name of a tribal progenitor. The English counterpart would be "House of", for example the House of Saud. Another example of the usage is the Bani Quraish, the tribe from which Muhammad came, for which a Quranic Surah is also named \[...\] For example, "Bani Kaab" literally means the sons of Kaab: the house of Kaab.  
    There are also a number of sages with patronyms that are likely clan/family names, based on a) rarity of name; 2\) comparison with names in late Biblical books (cf. my piece on patronyms that aren’t father’s names; and my pieces on Jewish clans in Second Temple times [here](https://www.ezrabrand.com/p/the-eight-second-temple-clans-in) and [here](https://www.ezrabrand.com/p/fifteen-named-officials-who-served); on official priestly clans/families/divisions \[משמרות כהונה\], see [here](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%9B%D7%94%D7%95%D7%A0%D7%94) and [here](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priestly_divisions); and see also Shmuel Klein, p. 328-349, on family names (שם האבות) in rabbinic literature):

    1.  בן אלינתן \- 'אלנתן' הוא [שם בתנ"ך](https://search.dicta.org.il/result?text=%D7%90%D7%9C%D7%A0%D7%AA%D7%9F) \- ("רבי נחוניא בן אלינתן איש כפר הבבלי" \- [Mishnah\_Eduyot.6.2](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Mishnah_Eduyot.6.2))  
    2. בן עזריה (רבי אליעזר בן עזריה)  
    3. בן הורקנוס / הרכינס (רבי אליעזר בן הורקנוס; [רבי דוסא בן הרכינס](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%93%D7%95%D7%A1%D7%90_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%94%D7%A8%D7%9B%D7%99%D7%A0%D7%A1))  
    4. בן משולם ([רבי יוסי בן \[ה\]משולם](https://www.hamichlol.org.il/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%99%D7%95%D7%A1%D7%99_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%9E%D7%A9%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%9D) ; רבי יוחנן בן \[המשולם\])  
    5. [בית אלישיב](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%90%D7%9C%D7%99%D7%A9%D7%99%D7%91) (משמרת אלישיב \- [I\_Chronicles.24.12](https://www.sefaria.org.il/I_Chronicles.24.12); "לשכת יהוחנן בן אלישיב" \- [Ezra.10.6](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Ezra.10.6) ; [רבי יצחק בר/בן אלישיב](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%99%D7%A6%D7%97%D7%A7_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%90%D7%9C%D7%99%D7%A9%D7%99%D7%91) \- [Jerusalem\_Talmud\_Maaser\_Sheni.2.2.8](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jerusalem_Talmud_Maaser_Sheni.2.2.8); בי בר אלישיב)   
    6. בן זכאי / זכיי (רבי יוחנן בן זכאי)

    8. בית בלגה ([משמרת בלגה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%AA_%D7%91%D7%99%D7%9C%D7%92%D7%94) \- [I\_Chronicles.24.14](https://www.sefaria.org.il/I_Chronicles.24.14) ; מרים בת בלגה)  
    9. [בית בייתוס](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA_%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%A1) ([מרתא בת בייתוס](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%AA%D7%90_%D7%91%D7%AA_%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%A1))   
    10. בית יקים ([משמרת יקים](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%AA_%D7%99%D7%A7%D7%99%D7%9D) ; רבי שמעון בן/בר יקים)

    13. [בית קמחית](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%91%D7%99%D7%AA_%D7%A7%D7%9E%D7%97%D7%99%D7%AA) (רבי ישמעאל בן קמחית \- [Yoma.47a.11](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Yoma.47a.11); ישבב \[בן קמחית\] (שם); בירושלמי ומדרש: שמעון בן קמחית \- [Vayikra\_Rabbah.20.11](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Vayikra_Rabbah.20.11) ; יהודה \[בן קמחית\] \- [Jerusalem\_Talmud\_Yoma.1.1.23](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Jerusalem_Talmud_Yoma.1.1.23))

[^136]:  There’s a traditionalist tendency to provide names where it’s missing. See the extensive list in Benizri, “Seder”, pp. 111-114 (“סימני זיהוי התנאים והאמוראים”). Ornstein in general systematically assumes that anonymous names were hidden on purpose. See his pronouncement in a footnote on the term ההוא סבא (p. 888, footnote 1). See also ibid. (p. 859ff) his speculation regarding the term ההוא כובס \- “a certain launderer”, as a codename throughout the Talmud for an agent of the Nasi. (Cf. TTA \- [תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/כ/כובס – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%9B/%D7%9B%D7%95%D7%91%D7%A1)) However, the reasons he gives come from a more traditionalist perspective, and are not convincing from a critical perspective.  
    For a further illustration of the traditionalist tendency to provide names where it’s missing, compare the entry on “List of names for the Biblical nameless”, with discussion and sources:   
    [List of names for the biblical nameless \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_names_for_the_biblical_nameless)

[^149]:  אורנשטיין, עמ' 2269 ואילך.   
    On *rabbi* vs. *rav*, see Norman Solomon, *Talmud,* in his introduction:   
    “Sherira claimed that the Talmud applies the title ‘Rabbi’ to scholars in the Land of Israel and ‘Rav’ to Babylonians, and in a letter to the community of Kairouan he wrote, ‘Rabbi is greater than Rav, Rabban is greater than Rabbi, the untitled name is greater than Rabban.’   
    Yochanan Breuer has disputed this traditional analysis, arguing that *Rav* was originally a dialectical variant of *Rabbi*, consistent with the Eastern Aramaic habit of dropping possessive endings; *Rabban* (as is generally agreed) was a title reserved for the Nasi, and the ‘untitled name’ by which Hillel and Shammai, for instance, are known is because they predated the use of titles.”

     וראו עדי בלוט, "[טבי עבדו של רבן גמליאל](https://midrasha.biu.ac.il/files/midrasha/shared/tby_bdv_shl_rbn_gmlyl_dy_blvt_mvdknpdf.pdf)", *דרישה*, 1 (תשע"ו), עמ' 4:   
    "בתקופת התנאים היה נהוג להשתמש בתואר "אבא פלוני" ו"אמא פלונית" כתואר של כבוד (בדומה לשימוש הנעשה כיום בכינויים: "מר פלוני" או "מרת פלונית").   
    כך למשל מצינו תנאים הנקראים אבא שאול, אבא בנימין, אבא חלקיה, אמורא הנקרא אבא אושעיא, אמא שלום (שהייתה אחותו של רבן גמליאל דיבנה) ועוד.   
    חכמים סייגו את השימוש בתארי כבוד אלה ואסרו את השימוש בהם לעבדים ושפחות \[...\]"  
    *TTA* also writes:   
    [תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/א/אבא (תואר) – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%90/%D7%90%D7%91%D7%90_\(%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%90%D7%A8\)): "\[...\] שם אבא הוא אך תואר כבוד".

    Cf. [Mar \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar) : “*Mar* (from Classical Syriac:\[...\] *Mār(y*), written with a silent final *yodh*), also *Mor* in Western Syriac has the literal meaning "milord", it is a title of reverence in Syriac Christianity. The corresponding feminine forms are *Morth* and *Marth* for "milady" (Syriac: \[...\] *Mārt(y)*). The title is placed before the Christian name, as in Mar Aprem/ Mor Afrem (Ephrem the Syrian) and Marth/ Morth Maryam for St Mary \[...\] The variant *Moran* or *Maran* (Syriac: \[...\] Moran),\[citation needed\] meaning "Our Lord", is a particular title given to Jesus, either alone or in combination with other names and titles. Likewise, *Marth* or *Morth* (Syriac:\[...\] *Mārtan*, "Our Lady") is a title of Mary, mother of Jesus \[...\] In Mishnaic Hebrew \[...\] this Aramaic word is pronounced \[*mar*\] (Hebrew: מָר‎), and it is used as a formal way of addressing or referring to a male person. \[...\] "*Mar*" was also the title of the Exilarch (leader of the Jewish diaspora community in Babylon), with the Aramaic-speaking Jews sharing many cultural attributes with the Syriac Christians.”  
    Compare also the name *Mari:*

[^200]:  [תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/א/איסי בן יהודה – ויקיטקסט](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%90/%D7%90%D7%99%D7%A1%D7%99_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%99%D7%94%D7%95%D7%93%D7%94) : "למה נקרא שמו קטנתא שהיה קטנתא של חסידים"  
    תלמוד ירושלמי מסכת בבא קמא פרק ג הלכה ז: "אמרי הוא יוסי הבבלי הוא יוסי בן יהודה הוא יוסי קטנתה ולמה נקרא שמו קטונתה שהיה קטן חסידים." אורנשטיין, עמ' 2169 ואילך.   
    Compare also שמואל הקטן.

[^201]:  כנראה רגיל במיוחד בחכמים המרכזיים שחיו ופעלו בשליש האחרון של המאה השנייה, שהיו בניהם של חכמים מפורסמים שפעלו בדור שאחרי מרד בר־כוכבא. ראו שרמר, "עולמם", עמ' 571: "רבים מן החכמים המרכזיים שחיו ופעלו בשליש האחרון של המאה השנייה היו בניהם של חכמים מפורסמים שפעלו בדור שאחרי מרד בר־כוכבא, כגון רבי שמעון בן אלעזר; רבי אלעזר ברבי שמעון; רבי יהודה ברבי יוסי; רבי יוסי ברבי יהודה, ורבים אחרים."

[^205]:  Similar to nowadays referring to somebody by their family name or nickname. Referring to a someone by patronym is found in the Talmud in contexts that are informal conversations. For example, [Meilah.17a.11](https://www.sefaria.org/Meilah.17a.11):  
    "אמרו לו תלמידיו: חכים ליה **בן יוחאי**\!"

[^210]:  “ת"ר: עד מתי הבת ממאנת? \[...\]   
    **בן שלקות** אומר עד שתכלכל.   
    ואמר רבי שמעון: מצאני חנינא בן חכינאי בצידן, ואמר: כשאתה מגיע אצל ר"ע, אמור לו: עד מתי הבת ממאנת?   
    אם יאמר לך: עד שתביא שתי שערות, אמור לו: והלא **בן שלקות** העיד במעמד כולכם ביבנה, עד שתכלכל, ולא אמרתם לו דבר?\!  
    כשבאתי אצל רבי עקיבא, אמר לי: כלכול זה איני יודע מהו, **בן שלקות** איני מכיר, עד מתי הבת ממאנת? עד שתביא ב' שערות” \-  
    [Niddah.52b.5-7](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Niddah.52b.5-7)  
    [תולדות תנאים ואמוראים/ב/בן שלקות](https://he.wikisource.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%95%D7%9C%D7%93%D7%95%D7%AA_%D7%AA%D7%A0%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D_%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%A8%D7%90%D7%99%D7%9D/%D7%91/%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%A9%D7%9C%D7%A7%D7%95%D7%AA)

[^232]:  [רבי יהודה בן פזי – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%99%D7%94%D7%95%D7%93%D7%94_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%A4%D7%96%D7%99) : "בעל סדר הדורות חוקר אם השם פזי היה שם אביו (או שם סבו) של רבי יהודה או שם אמו, אם שלא ניתן להחליט התשובה בבירור, ידוע כי אביו של רבי יהודה היה רבי שמעון בן פזי (או רבי סימון)."

[^233]:  רבי חייה בר מרייא (עסיס, "עוד", עמ' 229, הערה 296 ו \- 306\)

[^234]:  [אבא בר מרתא – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%90%D7%91%D7%90_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%AA%D7%90) : "בתלמוד, להוציא מקרה אחד, מופיע אמורא זה בשני כינוייו: "אבא בר מרתא דהוא \[=שהוא\] אבא בר מניומי". מהקדמת הכינוי בר מרתא, ומן העובדה שבמקום אחד הוא מוזכר בשם זה בלבד, נראה כי היה זה שמו העיקרי. לפי אהרן הימן, היו מרתא ומניומי אמו ואביו. לדבריו נקרא באופן חריג על שם אמו מרתא מכיוון שזו הצילה את חייו כמתואר במסכת יומא."  
    [רב יצחק בר שמואל בר מרתא – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A8%D7%91_%D7%99%D7%A6%D7%97%D7%A7_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%95%D7%90%D7%9C_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%AA%D7%90)

[^235]:  [רב מרי בר רחל – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A8%D7%91_%D7%9E%D7%A8%D7%99_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%A8%D7%97%D7%9C) : "בולט לעין צורת יחוסו, המתייחס אחר אבי אימו (שמואל), ולא אחר אביו כמקובל. הפרשנות המקובלת היא שאמו, בתו של שמואל, נשבית לגויים (כמסופר בתלמוד בבלי, מסכת כתובות, דף כ"ג, עמוד א') והרתה לאביו איסור גיורא, חייל בבלי בטרם התגייר, וילדה לאחר הגרות. וכך אמרו עליו בתלמוד: ”רב מרי הורתו שלא בקדושה ולידתו בקדושה הואי (=היה)”. בתלמוד ישנה סוגיה העוסקת בשאלה האם אדם כזה מוגדר כיהודי, למסקנה נפסק שכגר הוא נחשב יהודי לכל דבר \- אף למינוי של שררה למרות ההגבלה שנצרך שבעל השררה יהיה "מקרב אחיך" \- "מהמובחר שבאחיך", כלומר שאף אביו יהיה מישראל.  
    אמנם, מלשון אחת הסוגיות, על פי גרסה נפוצה, עולה שאביו היה יהודי ונקרא רבא, הגמרא מתייחסת לסתירה ומתרצת ש"תרי רב מרי בר רחל הוו" \- כלומר שני אמוראים היו בשם זה. אך רש"י והתוספות מציינים שהקושיה והתירוץ הם פירוש שנכתב בתוך הגמרא בתקופה מאוחרת, כדי לתרץ את הגרסה המוקשה. אך האמת היא שהגרסה שגויה והיה רב מרי בר רחל אחד בלבד, ושם אביו היה איסור גיורא."

[^236]:  [רבה בר בר חנה – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%94_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%97%D7%A0%D7%94) : "לפי חלק מהדעות היה רבה בר בר חנה בנו של רבה בר חנה, אך לפי אחרות מדובר באותו אדם, ושמו השתבש בתלמוד.”  
     רב יצחק בר בר חנה \- [Yoma.77b.8](https://www.sefaria.org.il/Yoma.77b.8)  
    [רבי מיאשה בר בריה דרבי יהושע בן לוי – המכלול](https://www.hamichlol.org.il/%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%9E%D7%99%D7%90%D7%A9%D7%94_%D7%91%D7%A8_%D7%91%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%94_%D7%93%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%99%D7%94%D7%95%D7%A9%D7%A2_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%9C%D7%95%D7%99) : "אביו נפטר בצעירותו, בחיי סבו רבי יהושע בן לוי \[...\] עבר לגור אצל סבו רבי יהושע, ועל כן נקרא על שמו ולא על שם אביו."

[^239]:  [שמעון אחי עזריה – המכלול](https://www.hamichlol.org.il/%D7%A9%D7%9E%D7%A2%D7%95%D7%9F_%D7%90%D7%97%D7%99_%D7%A2%D7%96%D7%A8%D7%99%D7%94) : ". רש"י מבאר את הסיבה שהוא נקרא על שם אחיו: ”שמעון אחי עזריה תנא הוא במשנה קמייתא דזבחים ולמד תורה על ידי אחיו שהיה עוסק בפרקמטיא כדי שיחלוק בזכות למודו של שמעון לכך הוא נקרא על שם עזריה אחיו”."  
    Is there a source for this, or did Rashi come up with this to explain the unusual surname based on brother?

[^241]:  [חנניה בן אחי רבי יהושע – ויקיפדיה](https://he.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D7%97%D7%A0%D7%A0%D7%99%D7%94_%D7%91%D7%9F_%D7%90%D7%97%D7%99_%D7%A8%D7%91%D7%99_%D7%99%D7%94%D7%95%D7%A9%D7%A2)  
    And cf. this interesting passage ([Sanhedrin.5a.12](https://www.sefaria.org/Sanhedrin.5a.12)):   
    "מ"ש למר, דקא קרי "בן אחי", ומ"ש למר, דקא קרי "בן אחותי"?  
    וכי תימא הכי הוה מעשה, והאמר מר: איבו, וחנה, ושילא, ומרתא, ורבי חייא, כולהו בני אבא בר אחא כרסלא מכפרי הוו?\!  
    רב בר אחוה דהוה בר אחתיה, רבה בר חנה בר אחוה דלאו בר אחתיה."

[^246]: Interesting speculation:   
    [שמות התנאים והאמוראים \- עמוד 2 \- פורום אוצר החכמה](https://forum.otzar.org/viewtopic.php?f=7&t=20644&sid=cb7df39c8f01cfe454439df35fbc3757&start=40): "האם נכתב משהו על התופעה שדוקא תנאים בהשם 'שמעון' יש כמות גדולה יחסית שצירפה אל שמותם שם לואי ע"ש מקומם או שם עסקם הנה הרשימה שלקטתי \[...\] אולי היתה השם שמעון נפוצה ביותר בזמן התנאים ולכן הצריכו להוסיף שם לואי לסימן?"  
    Shimon was indeed one of the most popular names in the Tannaitic period: [Simon (given name) \- Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_\(given_name\)), citing Ilan, pp. 56–57; Hachili, pp. 113–115; Bauckham (2017), *Jesus and the Eyewitnesses*, p. 71: “15.6% of men bore one of the two most popular male names, Simon and Joseph.”
